ParlaMint-DK_20210415102026	The sitting is resumed. I do not see Mrs Pia Kjærsgaard. We're gonna wait a little while to get started. Then all the rapporteurs are here, and that is why we can get started. The debate is open. It's the Minister of Business.
ParlaMint-DK_20210415102616	Thank you for that, and thank the authors of this motion for a resolution. It is a proposal which, if adopted, will mean that private individuals will be allowed to fire fireworks only on New Year's Eve and that the period for the purchase and sale of fireworks will be limited to a period from 26 December to New Year's Eve, that is to say 31 December. The aim of the proposal is to limit the nuisance caused by firing fireworks for domestic and pet animals, and the proposal is therefore put forward, according to the proposers, to increase animal welfare in Denmark. On 23 March, in fact, we debated in this Chamber a citizens' proposal to ban fireworks for private individuals. In this context, there was almost a broad consensus that we in Denmark should be able to launch fireworks when we celebrate the New Year. It is still my view that we must uphold the tradition we have in Denmark to celebrate the New Year with fireworks in the sky. I know it's a celebration that a lot of people appreciate and enjoy that way too, but it's of course important that we deal with fireworks with both caution and respect, because in addition to caring for each other, we also have to take account of our animals and their welfare. In 2014, stricter rules were introduced for the sale and use of fireworks. This was done in order to ensure a better balance between those who rejoice in celebrating the New Year with fireworks and those who feel bothered by fireworks. Before 2014, the period of application of fireworks was from 1 December until 5 January. Before 2014, the sales period started at the same time, i.e. on 1 December. Today fireworks can be purchased from 15 December to 31 December, and the period of application is also down, so it is now only from 27 December to 1 January. It is no coincidence that today there is a difference between the Danish sales and firing period. This is a well-considered decision and is due to the fact that different considerations must be balanced against each other. The 15 December sales period offers consumers good and flexible opportunities to buy fireworks. The sales period has also been determined in the interests of operators at the points of sale who have to meet a number of safety requirements such as: storage and transport. The sale period as it is now allows fireworks to be sold in a flexible manner. This benefits both safety, consumers and business. In addition, the rules in the fireworks sector also provide for a distance requirement of 100 m for buildings and land with livestock. It could, for example, be done. be for dog kennels or agriculture. It is very important that we continue to keep on informing and communicating effectively, both with regard to the applicable fireworks rules, but also with regard to behaviour, so that we ensure safe fireworks behaviour. It is also my clear experience that this is something that the vast majority of Danes, fortunately, can find out, and I would like to stress that the government is following developments in this area every New Year and is constantly assessing whether there is a need for adjustments to the rules or to the efforts of the authorities in general. So I have basically full understanding and respect that you want to limit the genes that fireworks can naturally cause, but at the same time I believe that with the austerity back in 2014, we have found a good balance, so there is room for those who like to buy and fire fireworks, and for them, and this applies to both people and animals who do not like it and who have troubles with it. Against this background, therefore, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415120728	Thank you. I used last year's debate, which we have already established four months ago, to puncture two widespread myths. The first is that we have equality in Denmark, and the second is that equality comes naturally. I hope and believe that most of us are beyond that: that autumn's many evidences of sexual harassment at work among many other things have made clear to even denies with the greatest blinds that we have a problem. Everyone must be able to see that equality has not been achieved. In addition, I am delighted to read in the statement that the government recognizes that equality does not come of itself either, because that myth has really lived and has grown up with changing ministers. But action is required, political initiatives. Both in the government and here in the Folketing, we have to roll up our sleeves and crunch the running shoes, because we have a marathon task ahead of us. Legislation is required if there is to be equal opportunities regardless of gender in Denmark. I hope that today we have buried those myths, and then let's move on. I have looked forward very much to debating perspectives and actions in the area of equality today, and there are really many good tactics in the government's perspective and action plan. I very much agree that we need to speed things up in order to create equality in education, in the labour market and within the four walls of the home; that equality is about freedom and rights, also for women under social control, which we owe it to help; that Denmark must continue its important focus and leadership in the field of gender equality, and especially at global level. When it comes to equal opportunities for lgbt+ people, the government shows an approach where you want to act and put in where the shoe prints. It's good to see. However, I have to say that I miss an indication that we will have a new and updated action plan in the area of lgbt+, because life must be easier for rainbow families. The road through the health care system must be less comfortable for people with a low level of health care, and people with a minority background need more support than they do today. But if there is one thing I think is more important than anything else, it is a reform of the maternity system. The earmarked maternity leave and the EU leave directive do not get much room in the statement, and I think that is a pity. The process of a new maternity-sharing bill will be extremely important in the year to come. We all know gradually that the single thing that is most important for equality and for the distribution of care in the family is maternity. That's the science bid. It's been a very, very long time. The sharing of maternity leave in Denmark is still glaringly biased. It is, although the Danish fathers would like to have more maternity leave if they could, but instead we have for decades reproduced gender stereotypes where mother cares for children and father makes career. We have missed female leadership talent and male father talent many times. This is despite the fact that our Nordic neighbours have given father maternity rights to his child. In Denmark, as you know, an EU directive was needed before we finally got the bag opened. I think that is really a pity, but that is what we now need in order to earmark maternity leave for men. In the next year we can then look forward to the work on implementing the Leaves Directive. We must succeed in this by August 2022, and I look forward to the discussions, because I believe that this will be one of the most important measures for equality in the labour market during this parliamentary term. It will be historic and something that our children and grandchildren will remember us for, but we must be careful. We must keep our tongues straight in our mouths, and we must remember the intentions of equality. The EU requires us to earmark nine weeks of maternity leave for each parent, and that sounds quite good on the surface, but it is not if it is implemented without a reform here in the Folketing. We could call it a minimum implementation. In that case, the new nine weeks of the European Union would be added to the already distorted maternity division that we have in Denmark, and then the imbalance will continue. It's headless if you ask me. The intention of the European Union is quite clear. There must be gender equality. We must promote women's participation in the labour market and we must have a fairer distribution of care obligations between men and women. None of these things will be achieved if you just add weeks to the already existing bias. In addition, such a solution will force women into the home for as long as 27 weeks if mother's new maternity weeks from the EU are added to existing ones. 27 weeks is a lot if you are self-employed, and this therefore puts women self-employed in a more difficult situation than male self-employed, and women entrepreneurs are therefore far too few already in Denmark. We should not allow ourselves to get rid of legal assessments that this cannot be done, that it is not possible to think again. We must not accept a possible tale that a reform of the Danish rules on maternity leave and an equitable distribution of the earmarked maternity leave under the Leaves Directive are not legally possible, because Finland has done so. They have blown up the entire Finnish maternity system on the occasion of the new EU directive and set in motion a large-scale reform of the country's maternity rules. The European Commission has stressed on several occasions that there is nothing wrong with doing so. Of course we can do the same here in Denmark. That is why it is so crucial that we show courage in the coming year when we have to implement the EU Leave Directive. I look forward to the discussions, and I hope that the government will use the open window that the European Union has given us wisely in a way that promotes equality rather than reproduces the inequality and the unequal sharing of maternity leave that we have today. I hope that the Danish Government is on the right side of the story here, because there are decades between us changing the rules on leave and that means everything to Danish equality. The question is whether Denmark should once again be left on the platform as the only Nordic country that does not succeed in a more equal sharing of maternity leave, while the other rushes away for the benefit of families, for the labour market and for the economy. That is why I look forward to implementing the Leaves Directive with the government. I look forward to doing so in a wise way that promotes equality and a way that gives as much earmarked maternity to father as there is to mother. It is my great hope that the government with us in the SF will help ensure more equality through equal maternity sharing. Here I think I will end my speech because although there are a great many other important agendas: gender equality, social control, which I mentioned very briefly, men's equality, the proper implementation of the maternity directive in the eyes of the SF is the most important thing we will be doing next year.

We are one of the most equal countries in the world, we often say. I guess we're number one. 29 in the ranking, and the four to five top seats are occupied by other Nordic countries. We should be lying up there with them. I think they are showing the way, and there are many good suggestions for what we can do, but we are a very equal country. Yet we just have a pay difference of 12-14 per cent. between men and women in general in our society. A lot of it is based on maternity leave, but a lot of it is also based on this gender-separated labour market, which we continue to reproduce, where women get the caring work, and men have everything else, especially in the private sector, and then the two sectors or the two kinds of work are just not valued the same. And that's not because women have lower education than men. In fact, we see the opposite trend or they have less responsibility. Well, nurses stand with life between their hands every single day, and the sosu are with our older citizens who have built this society for us every single day, and it's their cornerstone in life. These are insanely important jobs for our social infrastructure, and yet they are paid lower than in so-called men's jobs with a corresponding length of training. Well, a teacher has the same length of education as a builder. But it is not valued the same, and one of the reasons we find in the civil service reform, and now we come back to what is my hobby-horse at the moment: the civil service reform of 1969, where there were some politicians, not parties to agreements, but politicians in here, and decided that women's work wasn't worth as much as men's work, that police officers were more important and had to be paid better than nurses, and despite all the progress we have made in the field of equality, the hierarchy still stands. Actually, the midwives have been pulled down, that's the funny thing about it, so it's just gotten worse for them. This was examined in 2010 by the Salaries Commission, which among others. concludes that the Equal Pay Act is respected. In other words, men and women get the same pay if they do the same job. ParlaMint-DK_20210415130732	Thank you for the floor, President. And also thanks for a really, really good debate so far with really, very many dedicated speeches. As several speakers have said, it is not really a very long time since we debated equality in December, based on the government's 2020 perspective and action plan. And that much hasn't happened since the last time. However, with the government's new perspective and action plan for gender equality 2021, we have set the track for the next year's work in promoting gender equality across ministries. The plan contains 57 equality initiatives across 13 different ministerial areas. For although we have come a long way with equality, which many, fortunately, also highlight, we are not in the end. Today, I would like to highlight the key challenges that are at the top of my and the government's gender equality agenda in 2021. Firstly, we must spread the fight against sexual harassment. Secondly, we must continue to fight against negative social control. Thirdly, we must create better opportunities for fathers to take more leave with their children. Fourthly, we must work to promote equal pay. Fifthly, we must support the inclusion of more women in management and boards. Let me start with the first. Sexual harassment is not a problem that can be isolated to strong women with long education, as we have heard most so far in the Metoode debate. The problem exists everywhere in the labour market across industries and relationships, but especially among young people, those with the shortest education and lowest wages. We know from surveys that the lowest paid are exposed seven times more often to abuse and harassment than the high paid. That is why there is a great deal of work ahead of us in this debate. We need to have a broader focus on the challenges, because that is the only way to bring about a real change in culture in the workplace. Last year, the government launched 14 initiatives focusing on enhanced prevention, detection and consequences in cases of sexual harassment both in the labour market and in educational institutions. I am currently engaged in tripartite discussions with the social partners on how to prevent and deal with sexual harassment in the workplace in the best possible way. The plan is that we will hopefully arrive at an agreement around the summer. Once the trialogues have been completed, I will establish a broad alliance across organisations and with relevant NGOs. It must ensure a sustained focus on cultural change also in the coming years. Another important fight for equality takes place among minority ethnic women who are subjected to negative social control. There are also several rapporteurs who have highlighted this in this Chamber today as a key point in the fight for equality. For them, we owe everything we can possibly support in their freedom struggle. Much is about helping women into the labour market. Non-Western women make up one fifth of those who have been on cash benefits 10 years in a row but account for only 2%. of the population. At the same time, we know that there are women being pressured by their husbands or family not to take a job. It is a problem and an equal opportunities problem for women that they do not enter society, learn Danish norms and values to know and earn their own money and not least experience the freedom it gives. In Denmark, the liberation of women started just when women entered the labour market and became financially independent of their men. The government's ambition from the requirement to contribute 37 hours of work if you receive public support is therefore also that immigrant women become an active part of Danish society. We must send out a clear signal that both women and men must participate in society and contribute regardless of their background and culture. The government will also work to ensure that fathers have better opportunities to take more leave with their children. We need to implement the EU directive on earmarked leave, and although I would rather not have seen EU regulation in this area, I agree that it is important to have a more equal distribution of leave. It will benefit both the children and the fathers about the mothers. For me it is very much in the interests of both children and men, and that is why it is quite a man-political agenda. Fathers continue to take only 10%. of the total leave, although we know that many fathers would have liked to have lasted more. And studies suggest that father's leave benefits both children's development and well-being, improves father's health and association with the child and also reduces the risk of divorce. That is why we need to give fathers a better hand when the distribution of leave is to be negotiated both in the family and in the workplace. I hope that earmarks can contribute to that. I expect, at the end of the spring, to call for negotiations on how best to implement the EU Leave Directive. But there is also a need for a cultural change, so it becomes natural for fathers to take leave with their child. That's why the campaign "Action Fars Orlov" in 2021 focuses on just that. After all, the lack of equal pay between women and men, which many people have touched on in this Chamber today, remains a central challenge. The pay gap between men and women is still approx. 14% across the labour market, and that is, whether you consider it in what is explanatory or inexplicable. The wage difference is approx. 14% The reasons are not simple. There are a number of rapporteurs who are also right about this, and it cannot be solved with a snap, which some at least seem to have the impression of. Part of the pay gap is linked to the fact that the labour market is very gender-based. Because we want a society in which both men and women have the opportunity to gain influence and sit on the table when decisions are taken and because it offers the best solutions for our society and business. ParlaMint-DK_20210415133729	Thank you. With the motion for a resolution we are debating today, Mr. Sikandar Siddique, sir. Uffe Elbæk and Mrs Susanne Zimmer to introduce a mandatory practice of anonymising applications to public posts. I therefore agree with the authors of the motion for a resolution that indirect and direct discrimination in the labour market must be combated, and let me make it clear that I am of course impressed if job seekers are sorted out because of either their gender or age or because their name is not Western sounding. It is quite wrong for me to see that this can happen at all, because it stands in the way of job seekers' development and manifestation of life, and because the employer misses valuable and skilled labour. The government simply finds it totally unacceptable if job seekers or employees are discriminated against in the Danish labour market. That is why I am also pleased that we have clear legislation in this area, which contains clear rules to prevent illegal discrimination on grounds of, for example. age, gender and ethnic origin. This follows, among other things. In this context, it should be noted that, in the context of the legislation, every employer must treat job seekers in the same way as employment and that it is an absolute principle that employers must not discriminate against them in a negative way, for example. in relation to the ethnic origin or sex of a job applicant. Of course, I have a clear expectation that all employers will comply with the law, including in the case of recruitment, just as I am sure we agree that it is always unacceptable when someone is discriminated against. In other words, both legislation and common sense suggest that there is no discrimination. Nevertheless, we have to note, unfortunately, that we are still seeing individual cases and investigations which show that there are challenges. However, the question is whether new legislation, as proposed, is the way forward. The regulation of employment in state employment is a matter for the Minister of Taxation, and I have been informed that there is already considerable protection in the public sector for job seekers, for example. under the Administrative Act and the Discrimination Act. At the same time, recruitment today falls under local management law. This means that it is the individual employer who has the right to choose who is employed. A demand for anonymisation of applications will impose constraints on local employers and will run counter to the principle that it is the individual employer who has the right to choose who is employed. I do not think that this is something that we, from the political side, should interfere with with further legislation. I would also like to stress that a requirement to anonymise does not alter the primary fact that state employers should employ the best qualified candidate. The state recruitment practice is based on the fact that an objective assessment of the job applicant is always carried out on the basis of the formal requirements of a job notice and that no emphasis is placed on e.g.: age or gender, because it is forbidden to discriminate and because it is common sense to employ the best qualified, but we cannot run from the fact that we can still see examples that this does not happen. However, I do not believe that the solution at this stage is new legislation, and I do not believe that the proposal, as put forward, will solve the challenges, as few people are employed on their application alone. It is my clear conviction that we will be the longest through information and dialogue and that we will have to be even better at delivering the message that it is stupid both for the individual job seeker and for the individual employer if it is not the best qualified candidate who gets the job. It is therefore the government's opinion that no new legislation should be introduced requiring anonymisation of job applications for public posts. Against this background, the government is recommending that the motion for a resolution be rejected. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415135126	Thank you for the floor, President. In the Left, of course, we want a society in which your name, your gender, your age and your nationality do not affect whether you will have the position you have applied for. It should and should only be your competences that determine whether you are the right person for the job. Fortunately, this is also true in most places, but unfortunately not everywhere. That is why we very much recognise the problem that lies behind the motion for a resolution which has been tabled here. We are very sympathetic to the debate we are now having in the Chamber, because this is an important debate. Indeed, it is not right that some people feel that they are not elected to different positions on the basis of quite different things from their competences. However, we do not believe that anonymisation of job applications for public posts is the right solution. This is mainly due to the fact that we in the Left would much rather use the resources to tackle the causes of the problem instead of treating the symptoms. Discrimination and unequal opportunities in the labour market are a historic and cultural problem, and it takes time to resolve this. It can't be solved with an administrative snap. Moreover, we in the Left are advocates of trust. We believe that those responsible for the recruitment process in the public sector are also the most suitable for the task, at least in the vast majority of cases. And if you, as a leader, still have a strong desire to opt out of candidates on the basis of their sex, age or nationality, you will be able to do so in the further application process if you implement this proposal, i.e. anonymisation of job applications. We therefore believe that we should look instead at how we can really improve conditions so that everyone has equal opportunities in the labour market, both in the private and in the public labour market. This requires that we change a culture and it is hard work. We need to be better at lifting each other and at making each other better without making ourselves worse. We must learn to believe in each other, and we must learn to believe in ourselves, across differences and not in spite of differences. This requires initiatives to celebrate women who celebrate men, older and younger. One of the initiatives aimed precisely at honouring women who inspire and lift others with their work and character, we find in North Jutland. Last year, a prize was instituted, the Kamma Prize, a prize which, in all modesty, I co-created. The Kamma Prize is awarded every year to a woman who has taken the lead in inspiring other women to pursue their dreams. The prize has already been shown to have created visibility around the very many cool and brave women in North Jutland who are helping to inspire others and pave the way for other women. It gives more women self-confidence to go after their dreams, it gives more women who help each other into influential positions, etc. That is why we must learn from each other, we must lift each other up, and we must not least see the value of diversity. And we need to focus much more on the debate on gender equality, which will help to raise awareness and thus also the ability to act to create more real equality. For that is the only way we can really support, for example, the UN's World Goal five, which is called gender equality. In the Left, we simply do not believe that anonymisation of job applications is the solution, so we cannot support this proposal either. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415142053	This proposal is, of course, neither violently revolutionary nor a magic wand, as was said, which, with one blow, will solve the problems we think there are with the diversity of our labour market, but it is a good proposal. There is nothing to consider in relation to the public employer, because there has to be a notice. There must be a notice when looking for employees in public life. This is the first important prerequisite, and I would very much like it to be extended to the rest of the labour market, but it is not on today's agenda. But the very fact of having a post obligation to say that there is a vacancy here is the first prerequisite. Then comes the next, and that is, what should it say in an application, and how do you select an applicant? After all, there is evidence that very many employers hire people in their own image. ParlaMint-DK_20210415144807	Dear colleagues, dear listeners. Today, we have on the agenda one of the great highlights of the Free Greens, namely discrimination in the labour market. For in Denmark, unfortunately, we are witnessing the systematic and structural discrimination of people. Every day and in different contexts people experience being chosen from because of their age, gender, race, skin color, religion, sexual orientation, disability or ethnic origin. And this also applies to the Danish labour market, unfortunately. Because in Denmark your name, gender or gender identity or expression, age, religion or ethnic origin can have a decisive effect on whether you land at the top. Yeah, because if you're called up for an interview at all in the first place. That sounds quite absurd, but unfortunately it is a reality. For your name is Fatima, Wuarga, Mohammed or Alejandro, or if you are over 50, or if you have a different sexual orientation, then your options are just worse than if you are Mette, Marie, Rasmus or Rune. If Fatima even wears a scarf, or if Mohammed is transgender, the possibilities further deteriorate. In other words, the problem has an intersectional dimension. A recently published Danish PhD concludes that minority women wearing scarf must send 60%. more applications than ethnic Danish women to be called for an interview. A minority ethnic woman without a scarf must send 18 per cent. several applications, and if you have a Middle Eastern name, you should send approx. 52% more applications than if you have a Danish name. We all know the discrimination that is taking place around our seniors. If you are a woman and dream of a leadership position, your odds are generally worse than your male colleague's. This fact is also reflected in Denmark's incredibly poor position on the Global Gender Gap Report in 2020, measured on women in management. There we have a seat that is not to shout hurry over: number. 102 The trends we are seeing here are actually illegal, because according to Danish legislation discrimination on the labour market is prohibited, whether direct or indirect discrimination. It's forbidden. And that's what we need to stop right now, and I can't imagine that or that at least it was when I wrote the speech that someone in here would disagree with it. To put an end to discrimination in the Danish labour market is of course not something we do over night. However, researchers point out that a place to start is implementing a practice on anonymous applications. With such a practice, we can increase the neutrality and transparency of recruitment processes. This will reduce the company's biased attitude towards candidates in the early selection process and ultimately provide new opportunities for access. For us in the Free Greens, it is therefore obvious that we should try to go that way in Denmark, and that is why we propose that it be made compulsory that all applications to public posts be anonymised. Secondly, the state should take the lead in the fight against discrimination and form a role model as an inclusive employer and as an employer who recognises and is open to the fact that discriminatory attitudes can influence recruitment in the state. And if there is a tool that could potentially contribute to less discrimination in the labour market, we naturally owe it to the citizens and society to use it. Thank you very much for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415145311	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Minister for Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20210415145331	Thank you for the floor, President. Today we are dealing with a motion for a resolution which we have been discussing in a number of shades since the last parliamentary elections, and it has been a discussion for a long time and which, of course, we welcome from the government. We do so both because it is important and because it contains many nuances. The motion for a resolution deals with the abolition of the provision of benefits based on the principles of mutual dependency of cash benefit recipients, early retirement pensioners and old-age pensioners. This is a motion for a resolution which is very much similar to the resolution that we discussed in this Chamber back at the end of 2019, namely B 34, which at that time gave rise to thorough discussions on the scope and, above all, the complexity of the issue. I read the present proposal in such a way that there is a desire to settle the part of the benefit regulation which causes the benefit of a cash benefit recipient or pensioner to be affected by the income of any partner. First of all, I would like to acknowledge the proposal put forward by the Enhedslisten and the Alternative. The government is very aware of the issue, which has recently also been raised in relation to citizens who have experienced set-off as a result of additional work during the Coronapandemic. We jointly found a solution to the acute problem which arose in an extraordinary situation and the solution involves a temporary relaxation of the rules when there are citizens who have made an extra effort during corona. It was important, and I am very pleased with that. With regard to the specific proposal that we are considering today, I would like to make it clear once again that we cannot equate mutual dependants with the regulation of benefits for married and cohabitants, pensioners and early retirement pensioners. The mutual obligation between spouses in the cash benefits system implies that everyone is responsible for supporting himself and his or her spouse in accordance with his or her capacity. Cash benefits are the lowest financial safety net, and the rules mean that you cannot get help from the municipality if your spouse has wealth or income that can meet the family's financial needs for support. It is the civil liability laid down in the law on the financial situation of spouses which is essential for the liability of a spouse in relation to the cash benefits system. The responsibility for providing for a spouse ends by separation or divorce. People who live together without being married are not responsible for supporting each other, even though they live together in the same way as married couples. In addition, today we have a tax system which is also geared in several areas to the principles of mutual liability, for example. couples can make use of each other's personal allowances, giving married couples a better opportunity to support each other. A great deal of work is currently being done by the Service Commission, which has been given the task of making recommendations for a new one and among other things. more simple cash assistance system. I would therefore recommend that we await the Commission's recommendations for a new system of benefits instead of discussing isolated, but very fundamental, elements of the cash assistance system. In the case of dependent spouses and couples in the pension system, there is no mutual liability in relation to the rules on retirement pensions and early retirement. Here, the pension is measured on the basis of a common income for spouses or couples to ensure that benefits are targeted as necessary. However, substantial deductions are provided for to ensure that the pension is reduced only in the case of a significant common income. In addition, the pensioner is always provided with an independent dependency, as part of the pension is granted independently of the income of any spouse or collector. For over 100 years, the starting point has been that there are advantages in being two about the household. The income of a spouse or a couple therefore has an impact on the level of support provided by the welfare society. The purpose of the rules is to ensure the appropriate and targeted use of the tax-financed services. Having said that, I know that the economy can be tight when you receive public support, and I can understand that it may seem unreasonable that one's co-life situation affects the economic situation. At the same time, we also constantly have to give priority to how we spend the money we manage on behalf of the community, because our social model depends on the contribution of all those who can and have the skills, and it is based on a principle that our services are targeted at those who have the least and who need the most support. In this way, we can afford a strong social safety net where we take care of people who cannot support themselves. However, I am aware that there is a problem which concerns many citizens and also politicians here in the Folketing. It is also always possible to discuss whether the right balance has been struck between the need to target public services on the one hand and the need to provide reasonable opportunities, for example. to have an income from work. However, as soon as we go in and relax on rates, deductions and rules of dependency between couples and spouses, it costs money, and no matter which way one chooses to go, it will require tough economic priorities and it will reduce the targeting of benefits. For example, the abolition of the phasing-out of benefits in the pension system as a result of any partner's income will result in additional post-tax costs, reflows and behavior of less than half a billion dollars a year, and that is not an entirely insignificant amount. Although I would like to acknowledge that the proposers have put forward proposals for funding, including being open to engaging in a dialogue on other funding, the issue is also basically about whether there is room for the required economic priorities, especially at a time when we are struggling with the economic consequences of the Coronapandemic, which is the government's primary focus right now. At the same time, it is also important to bear in mind that a total clash with spouse dependency will have some distributional consequences and thus not be of benefit to everyone. In fact, out of the total number of early retirement and old-age pensioners, a significant number of pensioners benefit from the common income regulation because they can take advantage of the partner's unused income deduction and, at the same time, as the proposal is very comprehensive, it is not something that the government can support at present. In the end, I'd just like to pick up. In the government, we are concerned that our services are targeted as needed, but we also recognise that it is always relevant to discuss whether we have found the right balance between the various considerations. Changes have also been made in recent years which improve the conditions of the pension system, especially for pensioners who are married or cohabiting. First, with the 2019 Finance Act and later with the agreement on the entitlement to senior pension for worn-out from the same year, when the deduction limits for the income of folk pensioners were increased and escalated as a result of the income of spouses or collectors were relaxed. Although I recognise the intentions behind the motion for a resolution, the government cannot vote in favour of the motion for a resolution. This is due, among other things, to the fact that it would be very expensive to complete the abolition of mutual and spouse dependency. A clash with both the mutual dependency and the spouse- and partner-dependence will mean that tough economic priorities will have to be made in other areas, and the government simply does not think this is a viable way to go. It is the government's view that this resolution is for spouse dependency. Because, as I have said before, it is always possible to discuss whether we have found the right balance. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415153838	I would like to thank the Enhedslisten and the Alternative for tabling the motion for a resolution, thereby keeping the debate on the situation of pensioners and cash benefit recipients at a standstill. Corona's been good for something. In fact, it has been a kind of stimulant of injustices in society. The pensions for partners' work have always been deeply unfair, but the penalty for taking extra guards on top of the epidemic opened many people's eyes, and indeed many politicians' eyes in this Chamber. And that's why we decide to release so-called corona-related work for set-off. That's good, but it's far from good enough. I hope that it was only the first baby steps on a longer journey that could lead to justice for pensioners. There is now a hole in the wall, and we can see the country on the other side. That is why the SF supports the motion for a resolution as a good point of reference, but only as a point of reference. Because the proposal also contains elements for which we are not ready, right now, to give top priority. Families, one of whom is retired, get considerably less out of work over a certain amount than other families. The same work, the same income, but with very different value depending on whether one is retired or not. That doesn't make any sense at all. We hear so often loudly moaning from the right about the payment of top taxes. In this situation, however, it is mostly a matter of ordinary workers' families paying 70%. to society, and here, strangely enough, there is not so much moaning from the right. On top of that, we must put the increased uncertainty and insecurity around how many bills the public sends and on what amounts. The soldier veteran Lars, whom I have talked to, is hard hit by ptsd and told me about the extra stress he experienced with uncertainty about family income, where the partner works as a cleaning assistant. Kirsten, who is a nurse and has a partner on early retirement, told about the year when her family received a bill of DKK 17,000 in December. As a starting point, the SF is in favour of seeing each citizen as self-employed, and this is already the case today when it comes to unemployment benefits, sickness benefits, resource development benefits, occupational pensions and SU. SF is a financially responsible party and we give the limited resources our priority. If we had a bottomless money tank, we would like to implement the entire package presented here by Enhedslisten and the Alternative. Unfortunately, however, economic reality does not look like that. We want to start with early retirement, and this is indeed a proposal that the SF has put forward in the past, since that pension is directly instead of a wage income. At the same time, but at least as a priority number two, pensioners must be freed from set-off. For pensioners, the set-off in relation to the partner's income starts already around DKK 220,000. We think that is a very low amount, and we could start by adjusting that limit upwards so that income dependency will decrease. For the recipients of cash benefits, the situation is a little more complex. Income dependence on cash benefit recipients is starting today at a ridiculously low level. As I recall, it already starts when the partner earns twice cash benefits, and that is where we are down in such small amounts when you already lose your income, i.e. cash benefits. Property limitation of DKK 10,000 doesn't make much sense either. Here we need to look at a fairer basis for a possible start of reduction. By the way, I'm imagining a phase-in over a period of time. However, if we simply abolish the partner dependency of cash aid recipients in a hurry, then this will create some dilemmas. To a certain extent, this will reduce the incentive to join an unemployment fund when you can receive 80% without prior employment relationship and membership of an unemployment fund. 60 per cent of the daily subsistence allowance, for non-parents and even less for young people. However, this will certainly lead to some citizens calculating what is best worthwhile and will thus opt out of the 'A' box. As I have already explained, the motion for a resolution contains a number of purely SF policies which we will have implemented as soon as possible. But there are also elements about which we are unsolved and therefore want to be examined further. And then there are parts that we are rather sceptical about, as the consequences for the cashiers and thus for our model for the labour market can be quite negative. SF is a financially responsible party and has therefore made a priority. However, we support the proposal as it puts the right bill.

You must meet with respect and dignity, even if you are affected by illness or unemployment. We should not have a society where you are sent begging with your hat in your hand and humiliated. No, we must have a community that makes each other stronger and ensure that social events do not lead to economic or social delusions, and we must ensure that a fair standard of living can be maintained even when we grow old. And in that vision it is clear that you should not be made financially dependent on your partner, instead economic autonomy must be guaranteed. However, despite the liberation of women, the struggle to have a welfare society and other rebellions, we have continued to frame in our legislation that today we can end up being made financially dependent on our partner. The regulation of benefits, based on the principles of mutual dependency, still applies despite several relaxations over the years, concessions that we have supported in the Enhedslisten, but now we propose, together with the alternative, that we abolish benefit regulation on the basis of spouse's or partner's income once and for all. In fact, many people are not aware of the fact that in Denmark pensioners and sick people who have had an early retirement because of serious illness and who cannot work, but who do not yet have the financial autonomy that we often pay tribute to, and are made financially dependent on their roommates or spouses, and in the same way with cash benefits, that there are more who are made dependent on the spouse. The treasure of love contributes to some of the Danes, who have the lowest income, having to pay fivefold tax. It is clear that we will get that answer out of SKAT in some way, but I would rather do it irrelevantly by abolishing all the partner-dependent services, and that is why we are standing here today with Enhedslisten and making this proposal. ParlaMint-DK_20210415174640	Thank you. It just doesn't change the fact that there are situations where the individual doesn't have the prerequisites for learning Danish. If you are mentally ill or if you are traumatized by war or have other reasons for not being able to attend classes on an equal footing with others, it is clear that you are lagging behind. It is not new to anyone that the Danish People's Party again wants to make it harder for the few people who have not been able to learn Danish at an adequate level. Already back in 2018, the Danish People's Party voted for a tightening in the interpretation area, which was to punish those who had not learned Danish at a high enough level after 3 years, considering that it is not reasonable for the Treasury to pay the interpreting assistance. But the problem with this approach is that in health care, for example, we are faced with a problem where the imperfection of Danish means that even the Medical Association can see that patient interviews affect the quality of health care. This makes the diagnosis more difficult and increases the risk of mistreatment. And if there is something that costs a lot of money in our society, it is maltreatment in the health service, not to mention how horrible it is if you cannot treat people for serious diseases alone because of a language barrier. We also know that the Danish People's Party has helped to reduce the Danish education that you receive when you come to Denmark. So I actually believe that in many ways the Danish People's Party itself is responsible for the problem they address here and which we fully agree with Mr. Morten Messerschmidt in is a problem. Instead of discussing a proposal that children should not be allowed to translate for their parents, we should consider a proposal to remove self-payment from interpretation again. That would solve one of the problems we have in the area of interpretation. We should also be given priority to a Danish interpretation programme, as history has unfortunately shown that it has not just been something we have been able to outsource to a single company, and there we have actually stood with the Danish People's Party to have the agreement terminated with those named EasyTranslate. After all, the Finance Act two years ago succeeded in setting up a working group to prepare a new Danish interpretation programme, and we look forward very much in the Enhedslisten to see what the outcome of that working group will be. It's probably been delayed because of corona as well as everything else. The quality of the interpretation needs to be raised so that we are sure that the mistakes we are seeing today will not be made. Let us have a certification system as we see it in, for example. Norway and Sweden, and let us ensure that people who have to translate in the health sector know about health and that those who have to translate in the legal field know about law and have legal understanding. The fact that children are being used by their parents to translate in certain contexts today is a direct consequence of changes in the law implemented by votes from among others. Danish People's Party. And the lack of attention to the quality of interpretation that we have suffered for many years also leads to this problem. I do not think there can be any doubt that children should not be put in a situation where they have to translate for their parents. I just don't think children should. But I do not think that we will solve that problem by making a ban. In my speech, I explained why it cannot stand alone and why there is a need to do just so much more than that, for example. to create a certification scheme and an interpretation training and to remove the user fee. The Enhedslisten cannot support this motion for a resolution.

The loans are proposed to be repaid by 1 November 2022 at the latest. Thirdly, it is proposed to extend the period during which the cap on the tax account has de facto been abolished until 1 May 2023. Of course, companies should not be forced to pay negative interest in the bank on any surplus cash when the government postpones payment deadlines. These three measures are a continuation of our joint efforts to get Danish companies and employees as well as possible through the crisis, which is why the Social Democratic Party supports the proposal. ParlaMint-DK_20210415182807	Thank you. What we are dealing with here is a resolution from the Alternative that wants to provide a number of tax rebates related to repairs and maintenance. To that extent, it can actually be a very sympathetic thought. The challenge and problem is that the proposed tax rebates are an expensive and inappropriate way of achieving increased consumption of repair services. Denmark is facing a marked green transition in the coming years. Firstly, the agreement on a green tax reform, which is intended to kick-start the green transition in Denmark and which has therefore been concluded by a broad majority in the Danish Parliament. It is an agreement that puts the green transition into a whole new gear and supports that we switch Denmark into a new green reality in the most cost-effective way. Secondly, there is the agreement on the green conversion of road transport, which is intended to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and at the same time ensure that more green cars are put on Danish roads. For, of course, we must be ambitious when it comes to the green transition and ensuring greater sustainability, but it must also be done wisely. There is the proposal for tax rebates specifically on repair services, in the view of the government, as I said, not the best idea. The government cannot therefore support the proposal and I would like to explain why. In concrete terms, the alternative is to reduce VAT from 25%. 12%. on small repairs of bicycles, leather goods and textiles. The Danish VAT system is characterised by the fact that all goods and services are initially charged 25%. VAT. Changing governments have held on to this, for which there are, in fact, a great many good reasons. It goes without saying that a single rate VAT system rather than many different rates is easier to administer, not least for companies, but also, of course, for the authorities. For example, reduced rates create. It is a question of defining problems, which makes it more uncertain to companies whether they are now imposing the right benefits and goods the right VAT rate. The immediate saving of a reduced rate of VAT will be reduced by the increased costs of administering reduced VAT rates. Citizens will therefore not see the VAT reduction fully applied to prices. Furthermore, a reduced VAT on small repairs is not a particularly targeted instrument for promoting sustainable consumption, as it acts, if you like, as a price subsidy which is independent of whether it is actually best for the environment and the economy to repair a particular product. The alternative also proposes that, according to the Swedish model, a tax credit be introduced for repairs and maintenance of household appliances carried out in their own homes. The Government is not in favour of such a proposal which provides for support for private purchases of repair services of a particular type of product from a specific industry. This also applies if the deduction is intended to be introduced through the rules of the housing job scheme. It is therefore not clear that the introduction of deductions for repairs to electrical appliances will result in less resource consumption. Nor can there be any justification for the deduction for repairs to electrical appliances on account of the fact that this is an area where there is considered to be a very high level of undeclared work. Finally, I also think I have to point out that this proposal, depending on the level of ambition, naturally also leaves a financial challenge which may well be quite large. However, although the intentions are actually good enough and the government shares the need for an ambitious green transition, it is important that we ensure that this is done in as cost-effective a way as possible. We're on our way. It will be an ambitious and also a long journey, but the goal is set, and with the agreements made in broad agreement here in the Danish Parliament, we are pleased that we have set a new green course for ensuring a more sustainable and green Denmark. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415183751	Thank you for the floor and thank you for tabling this motion for a resolution to reduce VAT on small repairs to bicycles, leather goods and textiles from 25%. 12%. and introduce a tax credit for the repair and maintenance of household appliances carried out in their own homes of 50%. of labour costs. The introduction of such differentiated VAT rates increases the complexity of the VAT system and leads to increased administrative costs for businesses and the tax administration. This does not mean that the lower VAT rate will be fully applied to consumer prices, as the immediate saving will be reduced by increased administrative costs. A reduced VAT on small repairs will at the same time act as a price subsidy which is independent of whether it is actually better for the environment and the economy to repair a particular product. Therefore, it is not a particularly targeted instrument to support more sustainable consumption. This would really be support for private purchase of repair services for a particular type of industry, and that would involve a distortion which the Social Democratic Party cannot support. In addition, it will complicate the housing job scheme and, since the use of undeclared work is not considered to be widespread in the area of repair of household appliances, it is also not possible to argue for any major gain according to the purpose of the housing job scheme. Finally, overall, the proposal will lead to a reduction in the total revenue of approx. 120 million kr. a year after re-entry and behaviour for which no precise financing has been provided. For these reasons, the Social Democratic Party cannot support the proposal. Thank you for the floor.

So put it down to zero instead, but a differentiated VAT just gives these boundary problems with when a bike is new and when it is used, and I don't think that's a good way to do it. So I actually believe that the solution is to a greater extent that one, for example. must set some product requirements so that you can actually separate the parts and have them used for something else. There may also be pawns on different things, so you actually put a deposit and get an economic incentive for you to deliver things so that the products can be collected and used on something else. So there are plenty of good solutions that you can use to support it, but my point is that the same tax system may not necessarily be the best idea to do it with, and that is at least my argument. So, although we agree with the intentions and think it is good that we should take up the discussion, I think it would also be good to do so in committees other than the Committee on Taxation, because that is probably where many of the other solutions I have mentioned lie.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415193431	Thank you, President. In the alternative, we will end use and throw out the culture and, of course, focus more on sustainability in our consumption patterns. Since the advent of the consumer society in the 1960s, companies have increasingly speculated on producing products that do not have a long life, simply to keep business going. I remember when I was younger driving Volvo Amazons around, and they were impossible to get rid of, because they could simply drive forever. Finally, they were abolished, because nobody could make money from it. We have also seen examples of planned obsolescence where products have an in-house malfunction, which means that after a while they simply fall apart. It is clear that things are not going any further, because we are not a place where we can allow this kind of thing anymore. We need to address the problem and introduce measures that promote an economy and a society that is not based on use and throw away culture. We cannot continue to buy a lot of cheap things, which also means that we cannot afford the expensive quality things. In concrete terms, the alternative to this proposal is to promote two measures. Firstly, we would like to reduce the VAT rate on small repairs from 25% to 12%. on bicycles, leather goods, textiles, and we are open to expanding the product range. Secondly, we will introduce a tax credit for the repair of household appliances if the repair is carried out in our own home, up to DKr 12,000. per person. This applies to hobs, ovens, washing machines and other household appliances. Here, too, we are talking about developing the product catalogue. With the motion for a resolution, the alternative seeks to provide incentives to move from the more harmful consumption culture towards a circular economy. By providing tax rebates related to repairs and maintenance, we want to extend the lifetime of a number of product types that are typically discarded today when only a small part of the product or functionality is defective. Sometimes you are almost encouraged to buy a new bike, as long as there is something wrong with the pedal. In any case, my bike dealer urged me to do so, which I did not accept. By introducing lower VAT on repairs and maintenance of goods, we are providing an economic incentive that sends out a clear signal that we want a more sustainable and circular society. It will help to put an end to the habit of thinking. We're gonna go from use to use and keep. This means that we must produce quality goods that can be repaired and maintained for many, many years, and we must not just have a circular economy, but a circular society. What we were hoping to achieve with the resolution was to shift the focus to buying quality products that last and then prolong the life of the products. Who knows if New Citizens aren't really on the right track when it comes to clothes. Maybe in 5-10 years. We have two shirts, but in return they are so fat, and they last forever, and then you swap them after 10 years for two others that are equally durable and good. It may sound like a brave new world, but it may actually be the way we should, in other words, have some quality products that last right, for a very long time and which we want to repair and adjust etc. etc. But we are aware that these deductions that we are proposing here cannot be left alone, but we hope that it can start and supplement a debate on how to focus on use and throw away the problem. We tabled a resolution similar to this in 2017, where we received support from the SF and the Enhedslisten. The Radicals were positive, but chose to wait for a report on the circular economy of the then government, which then appeared without having any special initiatives supporting repairs, except for an increased repair limit for cars. We are now putting forward the resolution again, and that is because we are throwing out products like never before. I have read with horror the tables on how it just continues and continues, and it is as if the products last less time. Do you know the one with black jeans? They can't even be washed anymore. Half of them can't be washed, because then there'll be white stripes like that. I've seen more people in the Folketing walking around with them, so I'm sure some people know them. It is as if they are committed to keeping worse and worse. At the same time, this scheme has actually turned out to be you in Sweden. We've had our hands on Skatterverket today, and they were happy with the arrangement. The VAT rate has been reduced by half on 1 January, for example. clothes, bicycles and shoes, and at the same time it has been introduced that Swedes can deduct labour costs from taxes when appliances are repaired at home. ParlaMint-DK_20210415194224	The debate is open. Here you go, the treasure minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20210415194242	Thank you for that, President, and thank you for the floor. As I have said, new citizens have tabled motions for resolutions No. B 197 on the possibility of a loss deduction in previous years' profits. The proposed resolution will allow companies to deduct losses from 2020, 2021 and 2022 in profits from the years 2018 and 2019. According to current rules, these deficits can only be offset against future profits, and therefore they cannot be reversed and offset against previous years' profits. The proposal is justified by the fact that many companies in the years up to the covid-19 crisis have had good years with large corporate tax payments, but indeed, in 2020 and very well in 2021, losses are expected. The authors of the proposal shall state the reasons on which they are based. With the proposed access to set-off in previous years' tax payments, deficits should be eliminated so that companies can quickly recover fully from the crisis, the reason is the justification. There should be no doubt that the recovery of the economy and Danish jobs after the covid-19 has the highest priority for the government. We have just discussed here in the Danish Parliament another very large proposal for increased liquidity for companies of as much as 73 billion. kr. and is thus now up to having ensured additional liquidity for Danish companies and Danish employees in the order of approx. 560 billion kr. So here in the Folketing, together in a broad majority, we have implemented historically large aid packages for business. As I have said, billions of kroner have been set aside for, for example, wage compensation and compensation for fixed costs and lost revenue, and those measures, in addition to the measures I mentioned earlier, help to strengthen the liquidity of companies here and now. As I have said, we have also focused on keeping our hands on business investment and, at the same time, supporting the green transition with the green tax reform, which was under discussion under the previous proposal, from December 2020. Initiatives have been agreed which, overall, are estimated to reduce corporate taxes by approx. 6 billion kr. in the years 2021-2025. We have among other things. an investment window has been introduced, which means that companies will have an additional depreciation access of 16%. of the investments made up to the end of 2022, we have extended the temporary increase in corporate deductions for research and experimental expenses, so the deduction now amounts to 130%. Right up to 2020, and we have permanently increased the limit on when companies can immediately write off operating funds, as it says, from approx. 15,000 kroner. for 30,000 kroner. So we have already had, one can safely say, the big toolbox forward. Time will show whether there will be a need to extend some of the measures and whether further ideas need to be put on the table. However, the Government does not consider that New Citizens' proposals to offset losses in previous years' profits and tax payments are the right way to go. This is first and foremost because it will be associated with significant administrative challenges. It will not immediately be possible to support systemically with the tax authorities and therefore a manual record of the losses paid will be necessary. This increases the risk of errors and, of course, also the need for controls. The Danish Tax Office estimates that, where appropriate, the resource burden will amount to a total of between 125 million. kr. and 275 million. kr. in 2022 and 2023. The actual payment of the taxes paid, i.e. the tax value of the deficits, is estimated to result in less revenue of approx. 1.1 billion kr. in 2022 and c. 0.5 billion kr. in 2023 after, as we say, retreat and behavior. The proposers have not allocated funding to this small amount of revenue or to the management of administrative costs. Finally, I also think it is worth noting that the administrative challenges mean that the taxes paid will not be paid until 2022. This will therefore not help to solve the liquidity challenges facing companies at present. Nor will it, as for example. the investment window or the increased deduction for research and experimental expenses, increase incentives to invest and thus support what could be called future earnings and jobs. So the conclusion is that you don't have to cross the river to get water. If further action is needed, the government believes that there are both better and, of course, more targeted means to support both business and Danish jobs, and it is probably no surprise that the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415195118	Thank you for the floor, President. The authors of the New Citizens' motion for a resolution here, which we are now debating, want to allow Danish companies to draw losses created in 2020, 2021 and 2022 from business profits for 2018 and 2019. However, we cannot support this proposal, and this is because, from the outset, it cannot be supported by a system, that it will involve a high level of resource constraints and a high risk of errors in management, and that funding is not allocated to the substantial amount of revenue involved in the proposal. At present, Danish companies do not have the possibility to offset past years' tax payments and thus obtain the tax value of losses that may otherwise be included in corporation tax paid in previous years. On the other hand, we have rules that ensure that companies with losses in a given year can offset the losses in taxable income for later years. You can always set off approx. 8,8 million kr. in 2021 level of losses from previous years in taxable income of the year. In addition, losses from previous years may reduce the taxable income of the year by up to 60%. If we were to introduce the model as proposed by the proposers, the Danish Tax Office estimates that this would involve administrative costs of about 125-275 million. kr. in 2022 and 2023, because the proposal cannot be directly supported by the system. This will lead to a need for a costful system approach, which will only be operational by 2022, and a manual record of losses paid, which will increase the risk of making mistakes. That is why the Social Democrats cannot support the proposal. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415201625	The debate is open. The Secretary of State for Taxation.
ParlaMint-DK_20210415201641	Thank you for that, President. With the motion for a resolution, the Left proposes to change the conditions for obtaining approval as a general welfare association, so that small patient associations representing rare diseases can be approved in accordance with the rules of Section 8 A of the law on equations. If we look briefly at the rules in force, I would like to summarise them. The rules today allow individuals up to a certain amount limit to be deducted from the gifts they give to the association. The maximum amount of the total deduction for gifts to non-profit-making associations etc. is in 2021 at 17,000 DKK. per person. The right of deduction is conditional on the association's funds being used for general or otherwise non-profit-making purposes, as it says, in favour of a wider range of persons. The association must also be approved by the Danish Tax Office and report received gifts to the Danish Tax Authority. In order to be approved as a general welfare association, certain conditions must, of course, be met. These are set out in an Order from the Danish Tax Office, and they imply that the association must have more than 100 Gift Gift Gift Gifts and over 300 Members who pay the quota, and an annual gross income or wealth of more than DKK 150,000. Small patient associations for rare diseases find it difficult to meet the conditions for approval. According to the associations, this is due, among other things, to the fact that the few cases of the individual diseases mean that there are often only the very nearest members of the association. The government believes that it is sensible to consider whether a special rule can be made for small patient associations for rare diseases. The patients' associations perform a role of some general importance, since the diseases are rare and there is therefore less knowledge of the diseases. This means, among other things, that the suspicion of a rare disease is likely to arise late in the course of a disease. In the past, as the rapporteurs also know, I have indicated to the Danish Parliament that I have asked my officials to look at possible changes to the conditions, possibly in the form of a derogation rule, and of course I am still standing by that. I have asked the Treasury to continue the dialogue with the patient associations in order to find a workable solution that can be presented to all parties in the Folketing as soon as possible. I have also worked to ensure that such a solution can form the basis for a change of law in the next session. It is essential for the government that a derogation rule should, of course, also work as intended. The associations concerned have indicated that it is not enough to look only at the requirement for the number of members. Other conditions can also be a challenge for the associations. Therefore, we in the Ministry of Taxation, in dialogue with the associations, will continue to work on the conditions that are relevant to dispensing from. In addition, there is also a task of finding an appropriate and manageable definition of the associations to be accommodated by such a change. So the conclusion is that the government is positive about making it easier for small patient associations to be approved. However, this must be done in a proper, but also in a good and well worked manner, and we are already working on this in the Ministry of Taxation, as I have said. At the same time, it is important to stress that a change of law is required if the associations are to be helped as desired, and it is not realistic that a change of law can be implemented in this assembly and therefore, on this basis, the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415204313	Thank you for that, President. The Left has tabled this motion for a resolution to increase the employment allowance by DKK 1.000. e.g. with a view to strengthening the economic incentive to be at work. The government's starting point is that the tax should not increase for ordinary Danes, and therefore the government is not in principle opposed to raising certain deductions, especially where ordinary citizens experience a gain. Right now, we are in a particularly serious situation which requires special action. The situation of the covid-19 has had major consequences for the Danish economy and, as has previously been the case today, it is a clear priority for the government to restore growth. We need to get out on the other side of the health crisis as quickly and as well as possible. That is why the government, which we have already dealt with earlier today with a new proposal for increased liquidity of 73 billion. kr., adopted extensive relief packages, moreover together with the Danish Parliament's parties, to hold hands under Danish employees and Danish companies, among others. by introducing, as we did earlier today, interest-free loan arrangements for the payment of A tax, labour market contributions and VAT by companies. As I said, some 560 billion has been secured. kr. in liquidity to companies during the crisis here. As part of the Finance Act, the government has also facilitated the tax on persons. There are, among other things: introduced the possibility for employers to give employees free of tax vouchers for experiences in order to support an industry that has been particularly hard hit by the corona crisis. After all, as the rapporteurs also know, and as we have discussed in the past, the housing employment scheme has also been substantially improved, but indeed temporarily. ParlaMint-DK_20210415213936	This motion for a resolution is about rolling back an increase in the generational tax on family-owned businesses. This is a proposal that we in the Danish People's Party fully agree with. We helped put down the movement, which is why it is not surprising. I do not think that today's positions are so surprising. Because, as I have already mentioned, there are two things that determine what the tax will be. One is what you are taxed, that is to say the tax rate, and then the other is what you are taxed by, which is the assessment. The historical story in this is that Benny Engelbrecht, as tax minister, abolished what was called the property tax rate. This was a way to make a predictable assessment of a company, and then you had something like the one leg of the assessment: what you are taxed by and it then brought to the Treasury about 900 million. kr., I think it was. Then, when there was a blue majority, we agreed that we would not make it more expensive to make a generational change for a family-owned company. We then had the choice whether to introduce what is called the property tax rate, which is one leg, or whether to make a lower tax rate. We didn't choose to reinstate the property tax rate because it actually hit awkwardly. It was in fact to the benefit of large companies, those with very high goodwill, and a disadvantage for those who were small enterprises, i.e. craft enterprises and very small family-owned enterprises. Because it would be wrong, we chose to say that it would actually be more equal if the money were kept within the system. One may disagree that it should not be more expensive to be a family-owned company and to change, but we then chose to lower the rate. But then we put a little bit on top of it, because the property tax rate gave about 900 million. kr., and the tax relief is now 1 billion. kr. So there's 100 million. kr. In contrast, I acknowledge that. However, the aim was not, in fact, a huge reduction for the vastest, or that it is the largest companies that will benefit most. In fact, that was not the aim of it, and that was not the result of it. But you can also hear today that the debate is completely distorted by the fact that it is now the goal to give the wildly rich Danes a huge tax relief. No. You got more money in the box because you turned up the rating, and then we just chose to turn down the second leg, which determines the total tax. But the goal was not a huge tax relief. But now we have chosen from the red majority in the Folketing, and it is their right to raise the tax rate again. So now there is an unchanged tax rate, but again with the high rating. So now we end up with a higher taxation for family-owned companies of around 900 million. kr. At least. I think that is a pity, because the family-owned companies are therefore a basic family in our business community. So we really think it is a pity that, after all, we could not just have a factual debate about whether it is reasonable for family-owned companies to have to pay more taxes. This is actually the case if you look back at the whole process when Benny Engelbrecht chose to raise the assessment on the companies. So it is not a discussion about whether or not it should be tax relief. It is whether it should be a tax increase or not. Because that is what lies in the historical perspective. ParlaMint-DK_20210415215436	Thank you. With this motion for a resolution, the Left proposes that we roll back the treble of the tax on generational change in family-owned businesses. It is also part of the draft budget that we presented in the Conservative People's Party, and that is why we can also support the resolution. The proposal is, of course, a consequence of the fact that, unfortunately, the present government, as one of the very first, increased the tax on the generational change of family-owned businesses from 5% to 15%. with effect from 2020 and, of course, we want to roll that back. The family-owned companies employ six out of ten private employees in Denmark and account for more than 70%. of Danish exports, and they are crucial for Danish prosperity and welfare. These are both large companies and very small, local companies such as the United Kingdom. merchants, small craft businesses and small farms and many other small businesses, which are now able to look forward to very large bills at the turn of the generation because of the government's increase in taxation, and these are large bills that affect some of the companies. It is not unusual for a family-owned company to pay a million dollars now, even though the company's value is actually quite small, and we think this is disproportionate and has implications for the ability of companies to create jobs and growth. We do not think this is the right way to treat our family-owned companies, and we are therefore voting in favour of the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20210415215636	This generational tax is deeply damaging to Denmark. That's how short it can be said.
